Debiasing Random Oblique Projections for Subsampled OLS and Fast CUR in High Dimensions
Chengmei Niu, Sachin Garg, Michał Dereziński, Zhenyu Liao

Abstract
Random sampling is a fundamental tool in modern machine learning and numerical linear algebra for reducing the computational cost of large-scale matrix problems. Existing analyses, however, rely primarily on subspace embedding guarantees, which do not precisely characterize the statistical bias of nonlinear random oblique projections induced by sampling, which arises ubiquitously in subsampled least squares and fast low-rank approximation methods. Because (pseudo)inversion is nonlinear , these random oblique projections can be systematically biased even when the underlying sketch is unbiased, thereby introducing hidden bias into downstream least squares and low-rank approximation solutions. In this work, we develop a unified non-asymptotic theory for random oblique projections in high dimensions. We show that standard random sampling schemes generally induce a systematic statistical bias overlooked by classical subspace embedding-style analyses, and we propose a principled debiasing framework to correct it. We illustrate the power of the theory through two canonical applications. For subsampled least squares, we obtain sharp bias–variance characterizations, reveal previously unrecognized statistical suboptimality in widely used sampling schemes, and identify when debiasing yields provable improvements. For fast CUR decomposition, we develop a debiased approach with improved approximation accuracy. Numerical experiments further validate our theoretical findings.



1
Introduction

Many problems in modern machine learning (ML) and scientific computing involve data matrices that are too large to be stored or processed efficiently.
Randomized numerical linear algebra (RandNLA) addresses this challenge through random sketching techniques
drineas2006sampling
;
drineas2011faster
;
Drineas2012fast
;
avron2017faster
;
roosta2019sub
;
bollapragada2019exact
;
Lacotte2022adaptive
;
derezinski2024recent
;
halko2011finding
;
niu2025fundamental
.
Given a tall matrix
\mathbf{X}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}
with
n\gg p
, one constructs a sketch
\tilde{\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{S}\mathbf{X}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m\times p}
, with
m\ll n
, to serve as a computationally efficient proxy for
\mathbf{X}
in downstream tasks such as linear least squares, low-rank approximation, and iterative second-order optimization.
The sketching matrix
\mathbf{S}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m\times n}
is typically generated either through
random sampling
, which randomly selects rows of
\mathbf{X}
, or through
random projection
, which forms random linear combinations of its rows.

In this work, we focus on the random
oblique projection

\tilde{\mathbf{P}}

chi2021multip
;
chi2021aprojector
induced by
random sampling
, together with its associated residual projection
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}_{\perp}
, defined as:
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}=\mathbf{X}(\mathbf{S}\mathbf{X})^{\dagger}\mathbf{S},

\tilde{\mathbf{P}}_{\perp}=\mathbf{I}_{n}-\tilde{\mathbf{P}}
.

These operators are designed to approximate, and should thus be contrasted with, the orthogonal projection
\mathbf{P}=\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}^{\dagger}
onto the column space of
\mathbf{X}
, and its residual projection
\mathbf{P}_{\perp}=\mathbf{I}_{n}-\mathbf{P}
.
Random oblique projections arise naturally in a broad range of RandNLA methods, including subsampled least squares
drineas2006sampling
;
drineas2011faster
, fast low-rank approximation methods such as CUR decomposition
wang2016towards
;
ye2019fast
, and randomized optimization methods
bartan2022distributed
.

A common principle in the design of sampling schemes is to ensure that the subsampled Gram matrix
\tilde{\mathbf{X}}^{\top}\tilde{\mathbf{X}}
is an unbiased or nearly unbiased estimator of the full Gram matrix
\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}
, i.e.,
{\mathbb{E}}[\tilde{\mathbf{X}}^{\top}\tilde{\mathbf{X}}]=\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}
.
However, such first-order moment matching at the Gram level does
not
generally imply unbiasedness of the induced oblique projection
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}=\mathbf{X}(\mathbf{S}\mathbf{X})^{\dagger}\mathbf{S}
.
Because the Moore–Penrose pseudoinverse is nonlinear, one typically has
{\mathbb{E}}[\tilde{\mathbf{P}}]\not\approx\mathbf{P}
, even when the underling sketch itself if unbiased.
Existing analyses of random oblique projections (and consequently of subsampled least squares and fast low-rank approximation methods) rely primarily on Johnson–Lindenstrauss (JL)-style analyses
johnson1984extensions
.
Such results aim to establish that
\tilde{\mathbf{X}}^{\top}\tilde{\mathbf{X}}\approx\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}
with high probability and therefore that
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}\approx\mathbf{P}
in some (matrix norm) sense.
While powerful and easy to use, such guarantees are often too coarse to capture finer statistical behaviors central to the practical performance of subsampled least squares and fast low-rank approximation methods, as simple and fundamental as bias and variance.

In this paper, we address this gap by developing a refined non-asymptotic theory for oblique projections induced by random sampling, and illustrate its implication through the two canonical applications of subsampled least squares and fast CUR decomposition.

1.1
Our contributions

Our main contributions are summarized as follows.

1.

We provide in
Theorem

3.1
a precise non-asymptotic characterization of the bias induced by random oblique projections under general random sampling schemes, along with a principled debiasing approach.

2.

Building on this result, we first establish in
Section

4
sharp bias–variance characterizations for subsampled ordinary least squares (OLS), including a bias lower bound for classical subsampled OLS (
Theorem

4.2
) and an bias improved upper bound for the proposed
debiased
subsampled OLS, whose variance matches that of the classical solution (
Theorem

4.4
versus
Theorem

4.3
).
We further extend this analysis to randomized CUR decomposition (
Theorem

4.8
), deriving a debiased fast CUR method with provably improved approximation accuracy.

1.2
Related work

Our work connects to three closely related lines of research: subsampled OLS, sketching-based CUR decomposition, and recent analyses based on random matrix theory beyond classical asymptotics. We briefly review these directions below and position our contributions relative to prior work.

Classical subsampled OLS.

Subsampled OLS estimators are a classical tool for accelerating least-squares problems by reducing the problem size while approximately preserving the information in the original design matrix
drineas2006sampling
;
drineas2011faster
;
wangsketchridge2018
;
bartan2022distributed
. In addition to their computational benefits, a growing body of work has studied subsampled OLS from a statistical perspective, focusing on properties such as bias and variance
ma2015statistical
;
garvesh2016astatistic
;
derezinski2017unbiased
;
wangsketchridge2018
;
dobriban2019asymptotics
;
derezinski2019correcting
;
derezinski2019minimax
;
bartan2022distributed
;
derezinski2022unbiased
.
Despite this progress, existing analyses do not provide a precise characterization of the bias between the subsampled OLS estimator and the full OLS solution, except for when using specialized determinantal sampling schemes, which are computationally expensive
derezinski2017unbiased
;
derezinski2019correcting
;
derezinski2022unbiased
. A central difficulty is the lack of a fine-grained understanding of the oblique projection operator
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}
induced by random sampling. As a result, prior work typically relies on coarse bounds or asymptotic arguments that do not fully capture the mechanism by which sampling introduces bias.
Our work addresses this gap by leveraging precise characterizations of the oblique projector
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}
to obtain a more refined analysis of the bias in subsampled OLS estimators.

CUR decomposition.

Traditionally, low-rank matrix approximations are obtained via SVD-based methods, such as truncated SVD or randomized SVD
(
halko2011finding
)
, which operate in the space of matrix singular vectors.
In contrast, CUR decomposition constructs low-rank approximations directly from selected rows and columns of the data matrix
michael2009curmatrix
, thereby preserving structural properties such as sparsity, non-negativity, and interpretability. These properties are often important in applications such as signal and image processing
(
candes2008Introduction
;
elad2006Image
)
, recommendation systems
(
koren2009Matrix
)
, and big data
analysis
(
michael2009curmatrix
)
. Given a matrix
\mathbf{X}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}
, a key design choice in CUR decomposition is the middle factor
\mathbf{U}
. Two common options are

\mathbf{U}=\mathbf{C}^{\dagger}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{R}^{\dagger}
, where
\mathbf{C}
and
\mathbf{R}
consist of sampled columns and rows of
\mathbf{X}
, and
\mathbf{U}=\mathbf{X}_{R,C}^{\dagger}
, where
\mathbf{X}_{R,C}
denotes their intersection. The former is more robust but computationally expensive, while the latter is more efficient but can be unstable when
\mathbf{X}_{R,C}
is nearly singular. Classical approaches mitigate this instability via pivoting-based methods, such as column-pivoted QR and LU with complete pivoting
golub2013matrix
;
trefethen2022numerical
, which enjoy strong theoretical guarantees
gu1996efficient
but are often impractical for large-scale problems.

To improve scalability, recent work has proposed randomized sketching methods. For
\mathbf{U}=\mathbf{X}_{R,C}^{\dagger}
, pivoting is typically performed on a randomized sketch
\tilde{\mathbf{X}}
instead of
\mathbf{X}

dong2023simpler
;
park2025accuracy
, with oversampling shown to improve stability and accuracy
park2025accuracy
. For another choice
\mathbf{U}=\mathbf{C}^{\dagger}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{R}^{\dagger}
, existing approaches rely on the sketches of
\mathbf{C}
,
\mathbf{R}
, and
\mathbf{X}
to form estimators such as
\tilde{\mathbf{C}}^{\dagger}\tilde{\mathbf{X}}\tilde{\mathbf{R}}^{\dagger}

wang2016towards
;
ye2019fast
. However, existing analyses rely largely on subspace embedding arguments and provide only coarse guarantees, without precisely characterizing how random sketching affects the induced projection operators
\mathbf{C}\tilde{\mathbf{C}}^{\dagger}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{R}}^{\dagger}\mathbf{R}
. In this work, we develop a fine-grained analysis of these random-sampling-based estimators by precisely characterizing the associated projection operators, leading to sharper guarantees and improved approximations of
\mathbf{C}^{\dagger}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{R}^{\dagger}
.

Random matrix theory.

Our analysis is closely related to recent advances inspired by asymptotic random matrix theory (RMT)
couillet2022RMT4ML
, which studies the spectral behavior of large-dimensional random matrices
anderson2010introduction
. RMT-based techniques have recently been adopted in large-scale machine learning to analyze optimization and generalization properties
pennington2017nonlinear
;
fan2020spectra
;
mei2021generalization
;
couillet2022RMT4ML
. In particular, building on tools such as Stieltjes transforms, several works have developed fine-grained characterizations of inverse Gram matrices. Using Sherman-Morrison rank-one updates,
derezinski2021newtonless
;
derezinski2021sparse
;
garg2024distributed
;
niu2025fundamental
derive precise expressions for
{\mathbb{E}}[(\tilde{\mathbf{X}}^{\top}\tilde{\mathbf{X}})^{-1}]
beyond classical asymptotic analyses. Our work is closely connected to this line of research and extends these techniques to obtain a fine-grained characterization of the oblique projection operator
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}
under random sampling.

Notation.

Scalars, vectors, and matrices are denoted by lowercase letters, bold lowercase letters, and bold uppercase letters, respectively.
For a matrix
\mathbf{X}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}
, we denote
\mathbf{X}^{\top}
,
\mathbf{X}^{\dagger}
,
\mathbf{x}^{\top}_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
,
\|\mathbf{X}\|
, and
\|\mathbf{X}\|_{F}
the transpose, the Moore–Penrose pseudoinverse,
i^{\text{th}}
row, the spectral norm, and the Frobenius norm of
\mathbf{X}
, respectively.
We denote
\mathbf{X}\preceq\mathbf{Y}
if
\mathbf{Y}-\mathbf{X}
is positive semi-definite, and use
\mathbf{I}_{p}
for the identity matrix of size
p
.
For a random variable
x
,
{\mathbb{E}}[x]
denotes its expectation, and
{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}[x]
denotes its expectation conditioned on the event
\zeta
.
We use the standard Big-
O
and Big-
\Omega
notations, and
\tilde{O}(\cdot)
to ignore logarithmic dependence on
n
.

2
Preliminaries

In this section, we introduce several definitions that will be used throughout the paper.

Definition 2.1

(
Random sampling
)
.

For a matrix
\mathbf{X}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}
with
n\geq p
, a sketch
\tilde{\mathbf{X}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m\times p}
of
\mathbf{X}
can be constructed by sampling
with replacement

m
of the
n
rows of
\mathbf{X}
with a sampling distribution,
\{\pi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
,
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\pi_{i}=1
, and then rescaling by
1/\sqrt{m\pi_{i}}
.
This procedure writes
\tilde{\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{S}\mathbf{X}
, with
sampling matrix

\mathbf{S}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m\times n}
having only one nonzero entry per row.
One has
{\mathbb{E}}[\tilde{\mathbf{X}}^{\top}\tilde{\mathbf{X}}]=\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}
.

Definition

2.1
includes commonly used random sampling schemes such as uniform (
\pi_{i}=1/n
), row-norm-based (
\pi_{i}=\|\mathbf{x}_{i}\|^{2}/\sum_{j=1}^{n}\|\mathbf{x}_{j}\|^{2}
), exact and approximate leverage score sampling
mahoney2011randomized
, as well as hybrid schemes that interpolate between them, e.g., shrinkage leverage score sampling
ma2015statistical
.

Definition 2.2

(
Leverage score sampling
)
.

For
\mathbf{X}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}
of rank
p
with
n\geq p
, the
i^{\text{th}}

leverage score

\ell_{i}(\mathbf{X})
of
\mathbf{X}
is defined as
\ell_{i}(\mathbf{X})=\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}(\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{i},i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
.
The exact leverage score sampling refers to the random sampling approach in
Definition

2.1
with
\pi_{i}=\ell_{i}(\mathbf{X})/p
.

Definition 2.3

(
Importance sampling approximation factor
,
niu2025fundamental
)
.

For
\mathbf{X}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}
with
n\geq p
and a random sampling matrix
\mathbf{S}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m\times n}
in
Definition

2.1
with sampling distribution
\{\pi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
, the associated minimum and maximum
importance sampling approximation factors
are defined as
\theta_{\min}(\mathbf{X})\equiv\min_{1\leq i\leq n}\ell_{i}(\mathbf{X})/(\pi_{i}p)
and
\theta_{\max}(\mathbf{X})\equiv\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\ell_{i}(\mathbf{X})/(\pi_{i}p)
.

The importance sampling approximation factors in
Definition

2.3
, introduced in
ma2015statistical
;
niu2025fundamental
, quantify how a random sampling scheme deviates from the
exact
leverage score sampling in
Definition

2.2
,
In particular, one has
\theta_{\min}(\mathbf{X})\leq 1\leq\theta_{\max}(\mathbf{X})
with
\theta_{\min}(\mathbf{X})=\theta_{\max}(\mathbf{X})=1
for
exact
leverage score sampling.
As we shall see below, these parameters are crucial in our analyses of (pseudo)inverses.

Definition 2.4

(
Subspace embedding
,
drineas2006sampling
;
mahoney2011randomized
)
.

For
\mathbf{X}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}
with
n\geq p
and a random sampling matrix
\mathbf{S}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m\times n}
with
m\leq n
, we say that a sketch
\tilde{\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{S}\mathbf{X}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m\times p}
is an
(\epsilon,\delta)
-
subspace embedding
, or an
(\epsilon,\delta)
-approximation for
\mathbf{X}
if

(1+\epsilon)^{-1}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\preceq\tilde{\mathbf{X}}^{\top}\tilde{\mathbf{X}}\preceq(1+\epsilon)\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X},

holds with probability at least
1-\delta
.

The subspace embedding property in
Definition

2.4
ensures that, with high probability,
\tilde{\mathbf{X}}^{\top}\tilde{\mathbf{X}}
provides a reliable approximation of
\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}
.
This property has been extensively exploited in the literature to establish statistical guarantees for a wide range of sampling schemes
\mathbf{S}
; see
mahoney2011randomized
;
david2014sketching
.

Nonetheless, for many applications in ML and scientific computation, the subspace embedding-type guarantee in
Definition

2.4
is
not
sufficient.
As an instance, while one has
{\mathbb{E}}[\tilde{\mathbf{X}}^{\top}\tilde{\mathbf{X}}]=\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}
(as in
Definition

2.1
), the sketched matrix inverse
(\tilde{\mathbf{X}}^{\top}\tilde{\mathbf{X}})^{-1}
is
no longer unbiased
, i.e.,
\|{\mathbb{E}}[(\tilde{\mathbf{X}}^{\top}\tilde{\mathbf{X}})^{-1}]-(\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\|\gg 0
, due to the nonlinear nature of the inverse.

Remark 2.5

(
Inversion bias for Gaussian random projection
)
.

In the case of Gaussian random projection, with
\tilde{\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{S}\mathbf{X}
for
\mathbf{S}
having i.i.d. Gaussian entries with zero mean and variance
1/m
, the inverse
(\tilde{\mathbf{X}}^{\top}\tilde{\mathbf{X}})^{-1}
is known to follow the
inverse Wishart distribution

haff1979identity
with
{\mathbb{E}}[(\tilde{\mathbf{X}}^{\top}\tilde{\mathbf{X}})^{-1}]=\frac{m}{m-p-1}(\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X})^{-1}
.
As such, the scalar debiasing
\frac{m}{m-p-1}
is
exact
for Gaussian projections. Moreover, a similar
scalar
debiasing factor,
\frac{m}{m-p}
, is known to remain effective for both i.i.d. sub-Gaussian and LESS random projections
derezinski2021sparse
.

The
inversion bias
for random sampling has been characterized in the following result.

Theorem 2.6

(
Inversion bias for random sampling
,
(
niu2025fundamental
, Theorem 3.1)
)
.

For
\mathbf{X}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}
of rank
p
with
n\geq p
, let
\mathbf{S}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m\times n}
be a random sampling matrix with sampling distribution
\{\pi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
as in
Definition

2.1
and
\theta_{\min}(\mathbf{X}),\theta_{\max}(\mathbf{X})
as in
Definition

2.3
.
Then, for diagonal matrix
\mathbf{D}=\diag\{D_{ii}\}_{i=1}^{n}
the solution to
D_{ii}=\left(1+\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}(\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{D}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{i}/(m\pi_{i})\right)^{-1}
,
there exists
C>0
independent of
n,p
, so that for
m\geq C\theta_{\max}(\mathbf{X})p(\log(p/\delta)+(\log n/\loglog n)^{2/3}/\epsilon^{2/3}),~\delta\leq m^{-3}
, when conditioned on an event
\zeta
that holds with probability at least
1-\delta
,

(1+\epsilon)^{-1}(\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{D}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\preceq{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}[(\tilde{\mathbf{X}}^{\top}\tilde{\mathbf{X}})^{-1}]\preceq(1+\epsilon)(\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{D}\mathbf{X})^{-1}.

Theorem

2.6
shows that
any
random sampling scheme in
Definition

2.1
admits an
inversion bias
, and that the subsampled inverse
(\tilde{\mathbf{X}}^{\top}\tilde{\mathbf{X}})^{-1}
is close,
in expectation
, to
(\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{D}\mathbf{X})^{-1}
, as opposed to
(\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X})^{-1}
that one may expect from the subspace embedding guarantee in
Definition

2.4
.
It has then been shown in
(
niu2025fundamental
, Proposition 3.2)
that this bias can be effectively corrected, using a
matrix-level
debiasing approach, leading to a debiased sampling matrix
\check{\mathbf{S}}
of the same size, for which one has
\check{\mathbf{X}}=\check{\mathbf{S}}\mathbf{X}
and
{\mathbb{E}}[(\check{\mathbf{X}}^{\top}\check{\mathbf{X}})^{-1}]\simeq(\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X})^{-1}
in a spectral norm sense.

Since one has, for
\mathbf{X}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}
of rank
p
with
n\geq p
, that the Moore–Penrose pseudoinverse
\mathbf{X}^{\dagger}
satisfies
\mathbf{X}^{\dagger}=(\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{X}^{\top}
.
One should expect, as a consequence of the inversion bias in
Theorem

2.6
, that the random oblique projection
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}=\mathbf{X}(\mathbf{S}\mathbf{X})^{\dagger}\mathbf{S}
is also a
biased
estimator of the projection matrix
\mathbf{P}=\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}^{\dagger}
.
It is thus natural to ask whether random oblique projections can also be effectively debiased.
In the following section, we present such results.

3
Debiased oblique projection for random sampling

In the following result, we show that the debiasing sampling matrix
\check{\mathbf{S}}
introduced in
niu2025fundamental
can also be used to effectively debias the random oblique projection.

Theorem 3.1

(
Precise characterizations of debiased oblique projection
)
.

For
\mathbf{X}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}
of rank
p
with
n\geq p
, assume that
\min_{i,\|\mathbf{x}_{i}\|>0}\|\mathbf{x}_{i}\|^{2}/\|\mathbf{X}\|_{F}^{2}\geq n^{-\alpha}
for some constant
\alpha>0
. Let
\mathbf{S}
be a random sampling matrix with sampling distribution
\{\pi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
as in
Definition

2.1
, define the debiased sampling matrix
\check{\mathbf{S}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m\times n}
as
1
1

1

For rank-deficient
\mathbf{X}
(i.e.,
\rank(\mathbf{X})<p
), it suffices to restrict to the column space of
\mathbf{X}
(that is of dimension smaller than
p
) and
Theorem

3.1
naturally extends to this setting.

\check{\mathbf{S}}=\diag\left\{1/\sqrt{1-\ell_{i_{s}}(\mathbf{X})/(m\pi_{i_{s}})}\right\}^{m}_{s=1}\cdot\mathbf{S}.\hskip-2.84526pt

(1)

Then, there exists
C>0
independent of
n,p
so that for
m\geq C\theta_{\max}(\mathbf{X})p\log(p/\delta)
,
\delta\leq n^{-(3+\alpha)}
,
and
\theta_{\max}(\mathbf{X})
in
Definition

2.3
, when conditioned on an event
\zeta
that holds with probability at least
1-\delta
, the debiased oblique projection
\check{\mathbf{P}}\equiv\mathbf{X}(\check{\mathbf{S}}\mathbf{X})^{\dagger}\check{\mathbf{S}}
satisfies
2
2

2

The event
\zeta
captures the high-probability guarantee that for
m\geq C\theta_{\max}(\mathbf{X})p\log(p/\delta)
,
\mathbf{S}\mathbf{X}
is an
(\epsilon,\delta)
-subspace embedding of
\mathbf{X}
(see
Lemma

A.3
of
Appendix

A
), ensuring a well-behaved sketch throughout the analysis.

\displaystyle\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}[\check{\mathbf{P}}]-\mathbf{P}\right\|_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle=\epsilon^{2}\cdot\|\mathbf{P}_{\perp}\|_{F}^{2},

(2)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}\left[\|\check{\mathbf{P}}-\mathbf{P}\|^{2}_{F}\right]

\displaystyle=\tr\left(\mathbf{P}_{\perp}\diag\left\{\ell_{i}(\mathbf{X})/(m\pi_{i})\right\}_{i=1}^{n}\right)+\epsilon\cdot\|\mathbf{P}_{\perp}\|^{2}_{F},

(3)

with
\epsilon=O(\sqrt{(\log n/\loglog n)^{2}\cdot\theta_{\max}^{3}(\mathbf{X})p^{3}/m^{3}})
, for orthogonal projection
\mathbf{P}=\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}^{\dagger}
and residual projection
\mathbf{P}_{\perp}=\mathbf{I}_{n}-\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}^{\dagger}
.

Similar to
niu2025fundamental
, in
Theorem

3.1
we leverage leave-one-out arguments to “expand” Moore–Penrose pseudoinverses under random sampling.
However, establishing the bound in (
3
) requires controlling
{\mathbb{E}}[\check{\mathbf{S}}^{\top}(\mathbf{X}^{\top}\check{\mathbf{S}}^{\top})^{\dagger}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}(\check{\mathbf{S}}\mathbf{X})^{\dagger}\check{\mathbf{S}}]
rather than inverse moments such as
{\mathbb{E}}[(\mathbf{X}^{\top}\check{\mathbf{S}}^{\top}\check{\mathbf{S}}\mathbf{X})^{-2}]
considered in
niu2025fundamental
.
The former involves cross interactions between distinct sampled rows that cannot be decoupled using a single leave-one-out.
To address this issue, we adopt a refined non-asymptotic leave-two-out approach in the proof of (
3
).
The detailed proof is provided in
Appendix

E
.

Theorem

3.1
provides precise characterizations of both first- and second-order moments of the debiased oblique projection.
Precisely, we have that the debiased oblique projection
\check{\mathbf{P}}
is close, in expectation, to the true projection
\mathbf{P}
, up to an error of order
\tilde{O}(\theta_{\max}^{3}(\mathbf{X})p^{3}/m^{3})
for
all
sampling schemes.
Moreover, the seconder-order moment
{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}\left[\|\check{\mathbf{P}}-\mathbf{P}\|^{2}_{F}\right]
is also precisely characterized, as the sum of the leading-order term
\tr(\mathbf{P}_{\perp}\diag\{\ell_{i}(\mathbf{X})/(m\pi_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n})
and some error, also of order
\tilde{O}(\sqrt{\theta_{\max}^{3}(\mathbf{X})p^{3}/m^{3}})
.

Notably, it can be checked that for
\theta_{\min}(\mathbf{X}),\theta_{\max}(\mathbf{X})
in
Definition

2.3
one has
\frac{\theta_{\min}(\mathbf{X})p}{m}\cdot\mathbf{I}_{n}\preceq\diag\{\ell_{i}(\mathbf{X})/(m\pi_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}\preceq\frac{\theta_{\max}(\mathbf{X})p}{m}\cdot\mathbf{I}_{n}
, so that (
3
) further writes
(\frac{\theta_{\min}(\mathbf{X})p}{m}+\epsilon)\|\mathbf{P}_{\perp}\|^{2}_{F}\leq{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}\left[\|\check{\mathbf{P}}-\mathbf{P}\|^{2}_{F}\right]\leq(\frac{\theta_{\max}(\mathbf{X})p}{m}+\epsilon)\|\mathbf{P}_{\perp}\|^{2}_{F}
, with equality for
exact
leverage score sampling.

Remark 3.2

(
Scalar debiasing for exact leverage score sampling
)
.

Note that under exact leverage score sampling, where
\pi_{i}=\ell_{i}(\mathbf{X})/p
for all
i
(see again
Definition

2.2
), the debiased sampling matrix in (
1
) reduces to
\check{\mathbf{S}}=\sqrt{\frac{m}{m-p}}\mathbf{S}
, which coincides with the
scalar
biasing factor appearing for Gaussian, sub-Gaussian, and LESS projections in
Remark

2.5
.
In this setting, for the debiased oblique projection
\check{\mathbf{P}}\equiv\mathbf{X}(\check{\mathbf{S}}\mathbf{X})^{\dagger}\check{\mathbf{S}}
, the scalar factor
\frac{m}{m-p}
inside and outside the pseudoinverse cancels out, yielding
\check{\mathbf{P}}=\tilde{\mathbf{P}}
.
Consequently,
no
debiasing is needed for exact leverage score sampling.
One may thus expect that a similar phenomenon persists, at least to some extent, when the sampling scheme is close to exact leverage score sampling.
This statement will be made precise in the next section.

4
Application to subsampled OLS and fast CUR

Most existing analyses of subsampled OLS rely on subspace-embedding-type arguments and fail to provide precise characterizations of, say its first- and second-order moments due to random sampling
wangsketchridge2018
;
ma2015statistical
;
garvesh2016astatistic
;
dobriban2019asymptotics
;
derezinski2019minimax
.
In this section, we show how the debiased oblique projection in
Theorem

3.1
applies to establish sharper characterizations of the bias and variance of subsampled OLS (see
Definition

4.1
for their formal definitions).

Given a data matrix
\mathbf{X}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}
and a response vector
\mathbf{y}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, the OLS solution is given by

\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\mathrm{OLS}}=\argmin_{\boldsymbol{\beta}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}}L(\boldsymbol{\beta})=\mathbf{X}^{\dagger}\mathbf{y},~~\text{for}~~L(\boldsymbol{\beta})=\|\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}\|^{2},

(4)

which can be expensive when
n
or
p
is large.
We consider the subsampled OLS
drineas2006sampling
;
wangsketchridge2018
;
bartan2022distributed
solution:

\displaystyle\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=(\mathbf{S}\mathbf{X})^{\dagger}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{y},

(5)

where
\mathbf{S}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m\times n}
is a random sampling matrix as in
Definition

2.1
.
We also consider its associated
debiased
counterpart:

\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=(\check{\mathbf{S}}\mathbf{X})^{\dagger}\check{\mathbf{S}}\mathbf{y},

(6)

where
\check{\mathbf{S}}
is the debiased sampling matrix in (
1
) of
Theorem

3.1
.

Observe that
L(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}})=\|\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}(\mathbf{S}\mathbf{X})^{\dagger}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{y}\|^{2}
and
L(\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}})=\|\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}(\check{\mathbf{S}}\mathbf{X})^{\dagger}\check{\mathbf{S}}\mathbf{y}\|^{2}
.
Thus, subsampling replaces the orthogonal projection
\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}^{\dagger}
with the oblique projections
\mathbf{X}(\mathbf{S}\mathbf{X})^{\dagger}\mathbf{S}
and
\mathbf{X}(\check{\mathbf{S}}\mathbf{X})^{\dagger}\check{\mathbf{S}}
.
The deviation of these oblique projections from orthogonal projections governs the statistical behavior of the corresponding estimators relative to OLS.

In this section, we evaluate the statistical behavior of the classical subsampled OLS solution
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
in (
5
), the debiased solution
\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
in (
6
), versus that of the OLS solution
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\mathrm{OLS}}
in (
4
), per the following two metrics, in line with a series of previous efforts
bartan2022distributed
;
derezinski2019minimax
;
derezinski2022unbiased
;
garg2024distributed
;
garvesh2016astatical
.

Definition 4.1

(
Bias and variance of subsampled OLS
)
.

For a data matrix
\mathbf{X}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}
of rank
p
, a response vector
\mathbf{y}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, and the OLS solution
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\mathrm{OLS}}
defined in (
4
), let
\boldsymbol{\beta}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
be a random vector (such as the subsampled OLS solution
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
in (
5
) or its debiased counterpart
\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
).
Define

1.

Bias

\Bias_{\zeta}(\boldsymbol{\beta})=L({\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}[\boldsymbol{\beta}])-L(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\mathrm{OLS}})
as the deviation of
\boldsymbol{\beta}
from
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\mathrm{OLS}}
in expectation; and

2.

Variance

\Var_{\zeta}(\boldsymbol{\beta})={\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}[L(\boldsymbol{\beta})]-L(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\mathrm{OLS}})
that quantifies the fluctuation due to random sampling;

both measured by the square loss
L(\cdot)
in (
4
) and conditioned on a high probability event
\zeta
(which is necessary for many random sampling schemes, see
Footnote

2
).

4.1
Bias and variance characterizations of subsampled OLS with and without debiasing

We first establish in
Theorem

4.2
that the classical subsampled OLS
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
in (
5
),
without debiasing
, can exhibit a
substantial bias
(in the sense of
Definition

4.1
), at least under
certain
sampling schemes.
We then derive in
Theorem

4.3
an upper bound on the variance of
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
.
Finally, in
Theorem

4.4
, we present precise bias–variance characterizations for the proposed
debiased
subsampled OLS
\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
, demonstrating that debiasing not only mitigates the bias but also preserves, if not
improves
, the variance behavior.

Theorem 4.2

(
Bias lower bound for subsampled OLS
)
.

Let
p=4k
for any
k\geq 1
and let
n\geq 2p
.
Then, there exists a data matrix
\mathbf{X}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}
, a response vector
\mathbf{y}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, an approximate leverage score sampling matrix
\mathbf{S}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m\times n}
with
\{\pi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
satisfying
\ell_{i}(\mathbf{X})/2\leq\pi_{i}\leq 2\ell_{i}(\mathbf{X})
for all
i
, such that for all
m\geq p
and
any
real constant
\gamma
, when conditioned on an event
\zeta
that holds with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\Bias_{\zeta}(\gamma\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}})=\Omega(p^{2}/m^{2})\cdot\|\mathbf{r}\|^{2},

with
\Bias_{\zeta}(\cdot)
in
Definition

4.1
,
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=(\mathbf{S}\mathbf{X})^{\dagger}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{y}
in (
5
), and residual vector
\mathbf{r}=\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\beta_{\mathrm{OLS}}
.

Theorem

4.2
says that, under certain random sampling scheme (that is
1/2
-approximate leverage score sampling), there exists scenarios for which the bias of classical subsampled OLS
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
is
at least
of order
\Omega(p^{2}/m^{2})
.
Moreover, this lower bound is
not
limited to
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
itself, and holds for
all
scalar-debiased solutions of the form
\gamma\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
, for any
\gamma\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
This result stands in sharp contrast to the Gaussian random projection setting discussed in
Remark

2.5
, where an appropriate scalar suffices to eliminate the inversion bias, and thus the oblique projection in subsampled OLS.

The proof of
Theorem

4.2
(see
Appendix

B
) is based on the construction of a counterexample which imposes a non-uniform bias across the coordinates of the subsampled OLS, so that it
cannot
be corrected via a single scalar rescaling.

In the following result, we derive a variance upper bound for subsampled OLS
with debiasing
.

Theorem 4.3

(
Variance upper bound for subsampled OLS
)
.

For a data matrix
\mathbf{X}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}
of rank
p
, a response vector
\mathbf{y}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, and random sampling matrix
\mathbf{S}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m\times p}
with distribution
\{\pi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
as in
Definition

2.1
, there exists
C>0
independent of
n,p
so that for
m\geq C\theta_{\max}(\mathbf{X})p\log(p/\delta)
, any
\delta\in(0,1)
, when conditioned on an event
\zeta
that holds with probability at least
1-\delta
, the subsampled OLS
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=(\mathbf{S}\mathbf{X})^{\dagger}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{y}
in (
5
) satisfies

\displaystyle\Var_{\zeta}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}})\leq(1-\delta)^{-1}(\Delta(\mathbf{X})+\epsilon\cdot\|\mathbf{r}\|^{2}),

with
\epsilon=O\left(\sqrt{\log(p/\delta)\theta_{\max}^{3}(\mathbf{X})p^{3}/m^{3}}\right)
, for
\Var_{\zeta}(\cdot)
in
Definition

4.1
, and

\Delta(\mathbf{X})=\mathbf{r}^{\top}\diag\left\{\ell_{i}(\mathbf{X})/(m\pi_{i})\right\}_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{r},\quad\mathbf{r}\equiv\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\mathrm{OLS}}.

(7)

Theorem

4.3
provides an upper bound for the variance of subsampled OLS
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}

without debiasing
.
Furthermore, recall
\theta_{\min}(\mathbf{X})p/m\cdot\mathbf{I}_{n}\preceq\diag\{\ell_{i}(\mathbf{X})/(m\pi_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}\preceq\theta_{\max}(\mathbf{X})p/m\cdot\mathbf{I}_{n}
from the discussion after
Theorem

3.1
, the variance (upper bound) in
Theorem

4.3
can be similarly decomposed as the sum of the dominant term
\Delta(\mathbf{X})=O(\|\mathbf{r}\|^{2}\cdot\theta_{\max}(\mathbf{X})p/m)
and higher-order term of order
\tilde{O}(\|\mathbf{r}\|^{2}\cdot\sqrt{\theta_{\max}^{3}(\mathbf{X})p^{3}/m^{3}})
akin to
Theorem

3.1
.
The proof of
Theorem

4.3
is given in
Appendix

C
.

With Theorems
4.2
and
4.3
(on the bias lower bound and variance upper bound of subsampled OLS) at hand, a natural question is how the
debiased
oblique projection framework developed in
Theorem

3.1
applies to subsampled OLS, and whether such debiasing may incur an
decreased bias but increased variance
.
This question is addressed by the following result; see
Appendix

D
for the proof.

Theorem 4.4

(
Precise bias–variance characterizations for debiased subsampled OLS
)
.

For a data matrix
\mathbf{X}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}
of rank
p
with
n\geq p
and response vector
\mathbf{y}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, assume that
\min_{i,\|\mathbf{x}_{i}\|>0}\|\mathbf{x}_{i}\|^{2}/\|\mathbf{X}\|_{F}^{2}\geq n^{-\alpha}
for some constant
\alpha>0
. Let
\check{\mathbf{S}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m\times n}
the debiased sampling matrix in (
1
).
Then,
there exists
C>0
independent of
n,p
so that for
m\geq C\theta_{\max}(\mathbf{X})p\log(p/\delta)
,
\delta\leq n^{-(3+\alpha)}
, when conditioned on an event
\zeta
that holds with probability at least
1-\delta
, the debiased subsampled OLS
\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
in (
6
) satisfies

\displaystyle\Bias_{\zeta}(\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}})=\epsilon^{2}\cdot\|\mathbf{r}\|^{2},~~\text{and}~~\Var_{\zeta}(\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}})=\Delta(\mathbf{X})+\epsilon\cdot\|\mathbf{r}\|^{2},

with
\epsilon=O\Big(\sqrt{(\log n/\loglog n)^{2}\cdot\theta_{\max}^{3}(\mathbf{X})p^{3}/m^{3}}\Big)
,
\Bias_{\zeta}(\cdot)
and
\Var_{\zeta}(\cdot)
in
Definition

4.1
, and
\Delta(\mathbf{X})
defined in (
7
).

Comparing the bias-variance characterizations of the
debiased
solution
\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
in
Theorem

4.4
with those of the classical subsampled OLS
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
(without debiasing) in Theorems
4.2
and
4.3
, we find that the
debiased
solution
\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
yields a clear improvement.
Specially, it achieves a
smaller bias
(of order
\tilde{O}(\theta_{\max}^{3}(\mathbf{X})p^{3}/m^{3})
compared with the lower bound
\Omega(p^{2}/m^{2})
in Theorem
4.2
without debiasing), while incurring
no larger variance
(up to constant and some log factor), provided that

m\geq C\theta_{\max}(\mathbf{X})p\log(p/\delta)
.

For sketched OLS, it has been shown in
derezinski2023Algorithmic
that LESS embeddings nearly match sub-Gaussian sketches in variance, achieving
\Var_{\zeta}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}})=\frac{p}{m-p-1}\|\mathbf{r}\|^{2}+\tilde{O}(\sqrt{p}/m)\|\mathbf{r}\|^{2}
.
However, such a characterization remains absent for subsampled OLS under popular random sampling schemes such as leverage score sampling and SRHT, for which existing analyses are limited to variance bounds of the form
\Var_{\zeta}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}})=\tilde{O}(p/m)\|\mathbf{r}\|^{2}
; see
chen2019active
;
david2014sketching
.
The results in Theorems
4.3
and
4.4
close this gap by providing a finer-grained analysis for subsampled OLS, for both classical and debiased sampling schemes.
In particular, we show that the previously established coarse bound
\tilde{O}(p/m)\|\mathbf{r}\|^{2}
can be further refined as
\tilde{O}(p/m)\|\mathbf{r}\|^{2}=\Delta(\mathbf{X})+\tilde{O}(\sqrt{\theta_{\max}^{3}(\mathbf{X})p^{3}/m^{3}})\|\mathbf{r}\|^{2}
.

4.2
Precise bias–variance characterizations for leverage score sampling and SRHT

In this subsection, we focus on the two widely-used special cases of leverage score sampling and subsampled randomized Walsh–Hadamard transform (SRHT, see
Definition

4.6
below for a formal definition).
We show that the matrix-level debiasing in (
1
) is indeed
not
necessary (as opposed to general sampling schemes considered in
Section

4.1
), at least
to some extend
, for these two special cases of random sampling schemes.

Recall from our discussion in
Remark

3.2
that in the case of
exact
and close-to-exact leverage score sampling, our proposed debiasing approach in (
1
) also becomes a simple
scalar
rescaling.
As a consequence, one may expect that classical subsampled OLS solution
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
yields
small bias
in a similar spirit.
This is given in the following result, the proof of which is given in
Section

D.4

Corollary 4.5

(
Bias–variance characterizations for debiased subsampled OLS under leverage score sampling
)
.

Under the settings and notations of
Theorem

4.4
,
for
\mathbf{S}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m\times n}
the sampling matrix with sampling probabilities

\pi_{i}\in[\ell_{i}(\mathbf{X})/(p\theta_{\max}(\mathbf{X})),\ell_{i}(\mathbf{X})/(p\theta_{\min}(\mathbf{X}))]
with
\theta_{\min}(\mathbf{X})\in[1/2,1]
as defined in
Definition

2.3
, there exists
C>0
,
\alpha>0
,
\delta\leq n^{-(3+\alpha)}
such that for
m\geq C\theta_{\max}(\mathbf{X})p\log(p/\delta)
, when conditioned on the event
\zeta
that holds with the probability at least
1-\delta
, the standard subsampled OLS
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
in (
5
) satisfies

\displaystyle\Bias_{\zeta}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}})=\epsilon^{2}\cdot\|\mathbf{r}\|^{2},~~\text{and}~~\Var_{\zeta}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}})=\Delta(\mathbf{X})+\epsilon\cdot\|\mathbf{r}\|^{2},

with
\epsilon=O(\sqrt{(\log n/\loglog n)^{2}\cdot\theta_{\max}^{3}(\mathbf{X})p^{3}/m^{3}}+\epsilon_{\theta}\cdot\theta_{\max}(\mathbf{X})p/m)
,
\epsilon_{\theta}=\max\{\theta^{-1}_{\min}(\mathbf{X})-1,1-\theta^{-1}_{\max}(\mathbf{X})\}
,
\Bias_{\zeta}(\cdot),\Var_{\zeta}(\cdot)
in
Definition

4.1
, and
\Delta(\mathbf{X})
in (
7
).

Besides exact and approximate leverage score sampling discussed above, the subsampled randomized Walsh–Hadamard transform (SRHT)
ailon2006approximate
is another efficient data-oblivious sketching scheme.

Definition 4.6

(
Subsampled randomized Walsh–Hadamard transform
,
SRHT
,
ailon2006approximate
)
.

For a data matrix
\mathbf{X}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}
of rank
p
with
n\geq p
and a response vector
\mathbf{y}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, assume without loss of generality that
n=2^{p}
for some integer
p
.
Then, the SRHT of
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{y}
are given by

\tilde{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathrm{SRHT}}=\mathbf{S}\mathbf{H}_{n}\mathbf{D}_{n}\mathbf{X}/\sqrt{n}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m\times p},\quad\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{\mathrm{SRHT}}=\mathbf{S}\mathbf{H}_{n}\mathbf{D}_{n}\mathbf{y}/\sqrt{n}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m},

respectively, for uniform random sampling matrix
\mathbf{S}\in\mathbf{R}^{m\times n}
(with
\pi_{i}=1/n
in
Definition

2.1
),
\mathbf{H}_{n}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times n}
the Walsh–Hadamard matrix of size
n
, and diagonal
\mathbf{D}_{n}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times n}
having i.i.d. Rademacher random variables on its diagonal.
And we define, similarly to the subsampled OLS solution
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
in (
5
), the subsampled OLS solution using SRHT as

\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\mathrm{SRHT}}=\tilde{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathrm{SRHT}}^{\dagger}\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{\mathrm{SRHT}}.

(8)

Since
\mathbf{H}_{n}^{\top}\mathbf{H}_{n}/n=\mathbf{I}_{n}
and
\mathbf{D}_{n}^{2}=\mathbf{I}_{n}
, one has
\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{D}_{n}\mathbf{H}_{n}^{\top}\mathbf{H}_{n}\mathbf{D}_{n}\mathbf{X}/n=\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}
and a similar identity holds for
\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{y}
.
Moreover, the linear transform
\mathbf{H}_{n}\mathbf{D}_{n}
is known to effectively spread the leverage scores of the data matrix
\mathbf{X}
, in the sense that, with high probability, the leverage scores of
\mathbf{H}_{n}\mathbf{D}_{n}\mathbf{X}/\sqrt{n}
satisfy
\ell_{i}(\mathbf{H}_{n}\mathbf{D}_{n}\mathbf{X}/\sqrt{n})=\frac{p}{n}(1\pm\tilde{O}(\frac{\sqrt{p}}{n}))
, for all
i
, see, for example,
drineas2011faster
,
(
tropp2011improved
, Theorems 3.1 and 3.2)
, and
(
niu2025fundamental
, Lemma E.4)
.
As a consequence, SRHT can be interpreted as an instance of approximate leverage score sampling applied to the transformed data matrix
\mathbf{H}_{n}\mathbf{D}_{n}\mathbf{X}/\sqrt{n}
, and is therefore expected to be approximately unbiased per
Corollary

4.5
.

This intuition is formalized in the following result; see
Section

D.5
for the proof.

Corollary 4.7

(
Bias-variance characterizations for subsampled OLS under SRHT
)
.

Under the setting and notations of
Theorem

4.4
, let
\mathbf{X}_{\mathrm{HD}}=\mathbf{H}_{n}\mathbf{D}_{n}\mathbf{X}/\sqrt{n}
, and assume that
\min_{i,\|\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{HD},i}\|>0}\|\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{HD},i}\|^{2}/\|\mathbf{X}_{\mathrm{HD}}\|_{F}^{2}\geq n^{-\alpha}
for some constant
\alpha>0
.
For
\tilde{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathrm{SRHT}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m\times p}
the SRHT of
\mathbf{X}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{\mathrm{SRHT}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
the SRHT of
\mathbf{y}
as in
Definition

4.6
, there exists
C>0
,
n\exp(-p)<\delta\leq n^{-(3+\alpha)}
such that
for
m\geq Cp\log(p/\delta)
, when conditioned on an event
\zeta
that holds with probability at least
1-\delta
, the subsampled OLS with SRHT
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\mathrm{SRHT}}
in (
8
) satisfies

\displaystyle\Bias_{\zeta}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\mathrm{SRHT}})=\epsilon^{2}\cdot\|\mathbf{r}\|^{2},~~\text{and}~~\Var_{\zeta}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\mathrm{SRHT}})=\Delta(\mathbf{X}_{\mathrm{HD}})+\epsilon\cdot\|\mathbf{r}\|^{2},

with
\epsilon=O(\sqrt{(\log n/\loglog n)^{2}\cdot p^{3}/m^{3}}+\sqrt{p\log(n/\delta)}/m)
,
\Bias_{\zeta}(\cdot)
and
\Var_{\zeta}(\cdot)
in
Definition

4.1
, and
\Delta(\cdot)
in (
7
).

Table

1
summarizes the existing upper bounds on the bias of subsampled OLS estimator established in prior work under uniform sampling, leverage score sampling, and SRHT
wangsketchridge2018
;
bartan2022distributed
, and contrasts them with our results on debiased subsampled OLS.
We see that the proposed debiased approach consistently achieves a substantially smaller bias across all three sampling schemes.

:

Theorem

4.4
, under uniform sampling (

), (exact/approximate) leverage score sampling (

) with
\theta_{\min}(\mathbf{X})/(2\theta_{\min}(\mathbf{X})-1)\leq\theta_{\max}(\mathbf{X})\leq 1/(1-\sqrt{\theta_{\max}(\mathbf{X})p/m})
, and
SRHT
with
p\geq\log(n/\delta)
.

4.3
Numerical results for subsampled OLS

Figure

1
provides empirical evidence supporting our theoretical findings on classical and debiased subsampled OLS, by evaluating the effect of sketch size
m
on the (relative) subsampled OLS bias,
(L(\hat{{\mathbb{E}}}[\boldsymbol{\beta}])-L(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\mathrm{OLS}}))/L(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\mathrm{OLS}})
and variance,
(\hat{{\mathbb{E}}}[L(\boldsymbol{\beta}])]-L(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\mathrm{OLS}}))/L(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\mathrm{OLS}})
, with
L(\boldsymbol{\beta})=\|\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}\|^{2}
as in (
4
).
The data matrix
\mathbf{X}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}
is sampled from the Million Song Year Prediction Dataset (MSD)
bertin2011million
, and
\mathbf{y}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is the corresponding response vector.
See
Appendix

G
for implementation details and further experiments.

We see from the left panel of
Figure

1
that approximate leverage score sampling (
Lev
) and
SRHT
(see
Definition

4.6
) have
nearly identical
bias with and without debiasing, consistent with our
Corollary

4.5
and
Corollary

4.7
, respectively. Uniform sampling (
UNI
) exhibits the largest bias, while its debiased counterpart using exact leverage scores (
DUNI
) substantially reduces the bias, bringing its performance close to that of Lev, SRHT, their debiased variants (
DLev
and
DSRHT
), and the random projection approach LESS
derezinski2021newtonless
;
garg2024distributed
.
The right panel of
Figure

1
shows that Lev, SRHT, DLev, DSRHT, and LESS have
comparable
variances, whereas those of UNI and DUNI are slightly larger, with DUNI marginally lower than UNI.

Overall, we see from
Figure

1
that debiasing empirically reduces bias without increasing variance.
Similar trends are observed on the Flight Delay dataset; see
Section

G.3
.

4.4
Extension to fast CUR decomposition

CUR decompositions construct approximations from selected rows and columns of the data matrix
michael2009curmatrix
, which preserves structural properties such as sparsity, non-negativity, and interpretability.

Given a data matrix
\mathbf{X}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}
, a CUR decomposition is obtained
by selecting
c
columns of
\mathbf{X}
to form
\mathbf{C}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times c}
and
r
rows of
\mathbf{X}
to form
\mathbf{R}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{r\times p}
, and then computing
\mathbf{U}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{c\times r}
that minimizes the reconstruction error
\|\mathbf{C}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{X}\|_{F}^{2}
, namely

\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{CUR}}=\argmin_{\mathbf{U}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{c\times r}}\|\mathbf{C}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{X}\|_{F}^{2}=\mathbf{C}^{\dagger}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{R}^{\dagger},

(9)

as in line with
michael2009curmatrix
;
sorensen2016adeim
.
Although
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{CUR}}
in (
9
) typically achieves high accuracy, computing it requires pseudo-inverses and large matrix multiplications, which can be costly at large scale.

Note that the full OLS estimator in (
4
) is a one-sided counterpart of
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{CUR}}
in (
9
) under the specialization
\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{y}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and
\mathbf{R}=1
. Correspondingly, subsampled OLS in (
5
) improves efficiency by sketching only on the left. Motivated by the same idea,
wang2016towards
;
ye2019fast
proposed the
fast
CUR decomposition, which sketches both sides and computes

\tilde{\mathbf{U}}=\argmin_{\mathbf{U}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{c\times r}}\|\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{C}}\mathbf{C}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{R}\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{R}}^{\top}-\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{C}}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{R}}^{\top}\|_{F}^{2}=(\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{C}}\mathbf{C})^{\dagger}\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{C}}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{R}}^{\top}(\mathbf{R}\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{R}}^{\top})^{\dagger},

(10)

where
\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m_{c}\times n}
and
\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{R}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m_{r}\times p}
are sampling matrices; see
Algorithm

1
in
Appendix

F
for details.
As discussed in
Section

3
, the sketches in the random oblique projections defining
\tilde{\mathbf{U}}
may introduce systematic bias and could degrade the quality of fast CUR. Motivated by this connection, we extend our single sketch-induced oblique-projection analysis and debiasing strategy from subsampled OLS in (
6
) to CUR,
and propose the following
debiased
CUR solution:

\check{\mathbf{U}}=(\check{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathbf{C}}\mathbf{C})^{\dagger}(\check{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathbf{C}}\mathbf{X}\check{\mathbf{S}}^{\top}_{\mathbf{R}})(\mathbf{R}\check{\mathbf{S}}^{\top}_{\mathbf{R}})^{\dagger}.

(11)

Here,
\check{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m_{c}\times n}
and
\check{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathbf{R}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m_{r}\times p}
are the debiased sampling matrices as in
Theorem

3.1
.

The following result characterizes the bias of the proposed debiased solution
\check{\mathbf{U}}
.
Full bias–variance characterizations and their proofs are deferred to
Appendix

F
.

Theorem 4.8

(
Precise bias characterization for debiased fast CUR decomposition
)
.

For a data matrix
\mathbf{X}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}
, let
\mathbf{C}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times c}
and
\mathbf{R}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{r\times p}
be the pre-selected columns and rows of
\mathbf{X}
, and let
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{CUR}}
be defined as in (
9
).
For standard random sampling matrices
\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{C}}
and
\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{R}}
used in
Algorithm

1
in
Appendix

F
, define their corresponding
debiased
sampling matrices
\check{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m_{c}\times n}
and
\check{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathbf{R}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m_{r}\times p}
as in (
1
).
Then, there exists
C>0
independent of
n,p,c,r
so that for
m_{c}\geq C\theta_{\max}(\mathbf{C})c\log(c/\delta)
and
m_{r}\geq C\theta_{\max}(\mathbf{R}^{\top})r\log(r/\delta)
, when conditioned on an event
\zeta
that holds with probability at least
1-\delta
, the debiased matrix
\check{\mathbf{U}}
defined in (
11
) satisfies,

\displaystyle\Bias_{\zeta}(\check{\mathbf{U}})=\epsilon^{2}\cdot L(\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{CUR}})

with
\epsilon=\tilde{O}(\sqrt{\theta_{\max}^{3}(\mathbf{C})c^{3}/m_{c}^{3}})+\tilde{O}(\sqrt{\theta^{3}_{\max}(\mathbf{R}^{\top})r^{3}/m_{r}^{3}})
, for
L(\mathbf{U})\equiv\|\mathbf{C}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{X}\|^{2}_{F}
, the associated bias
\Bias_{\zeta}(\check{\mathbf{U}})\equiv L({\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}[\check{\mathbf{U}}])-L(\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{CUR}})
, in a spirit similar to that of subsampled OLS in
Definition

4.1
. Here,
\theta_{\max}(\mathbf{C})
and
\theta_{\max}(\mathbf{R}^{\top})
as in
Definition

2.3
denote the maximum importance sampling approximation
factors for
\mathbf{C}
and
\mathbf{R}^{\top}
, respectively.

In a spirit similar to Corollaries
4.5
and
4.7
, Corollaries
F.2
and
F.3
in
Section

F.2
also show that debiasing is unnecessary for fast CUR under leverage score sampling and SRHT. For completeness, we report the numerical results for fast CUR in
Section

G.4
.

5
Conclusion and perspectives

We develop a non-asymptotic framework for random oblique projections and reveal a systematic bias arising from the nonlinear interaction between random sampling and the Moore–Penrose pseudoinverse, a phenomenon beyond the reach of classical subspace embedding analyses.
To address this issue, we introduce a unified debiasing strategy that corrects this structural bias while preserving computational efficiency.
When applied to subsampled least squares, our theory yields sharp bias and variance characterizations and shows that widely used schemes, including leverage score sampling and SRHT, are statistically suboptimal in general.
For fast CUR decomposition, we construct a debiased estimator with provable improvements in approximation accuracy.

More broadly, our results highlight the need for bias-aware analysis in sketching-based algorithms.
Extending this perspective to other methods, including sketch-and-solve Newton algorithms, randomized preconditioning, and streaming low-rank approximation, may further advance the statistical understanding of randomized numerical linear algebra.

MD and SG were supported in part by NSF CAREER Grant CCF-233865 and a Google ML and Systems Junior Faculty Award.

Appendix A
Useful lemmas

In this section, we introduce a few technical lemmas to be used in subsequent sections.

Lemma A.1

(Sherman–Morrison formula)
.

For an invertible matrix
\mathbf{A}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times n}
and two vectors
\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
,
\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{u}\mathbf{v}^{\top}
is invertible if and only if
1+\mathbf{v}^{\top}\mathbf{A}^{-1}\mathbf{u}\neq 0
and

\displaystyle(\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{u}\mathbf{v}^{\top})^{-1}=\mathbf{A}^{-1}-\frac{\mathbf{A}^{-1}\mathbf{u}\mathbf{v}^{\top}\mathbf{A}^{-1}}{1+\mathbf{v}^{\top}\mathbf{A}^{-1}\mathbf{u}}.

Besides, it also follows that

\displaystyle(\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{u}\mathbf{v}^{\top})^{-1}\mathbf{u}=\frac{\mathbf{A}^{-1}\mathbf{u}}{1+\mathbf{v}^{\top}\mathbf{A}^{-1}\mathbf{u}}.

Lemma A.2

(Properties of the Moore–Penrose Pseudoinverse)
.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}
,
\mathbf{B}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p\times d}
. Then,

\displaystyle(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{B})^{\dagger}=\mathbf{B}^{\dagger}\mathbf{A}^{\dagger},

if any one of the following conditions holds: (1)
\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{I}_{p}
; (2)
\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{B}=\mathbf{I}_{d}
; or (3)
\rm{rank}(\mathbf{A})={\mathrm{rank}}(\mathbf{B})=\mathit{p}
.

Lemma A.3

(Subspace embedding for random sampling,
[
42
, Lemma 2.7]
)
.

Given
\mathbf{X}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}
of rank
p
with
n\geq p
,
let
\mathbf{S}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m\times n}
be a random sampling matrix as in
Definition

2.1
.
Then, there exists
C>0
independent of
n,p
such that for
m\geq C\theta_{\max}(\mathbf{X})p\log(p/\delta)/\epsilon^{2}
, failure probability
\delta\in(0,1/2)
,
\epsilon>0
, and
\theta_{\max}(\mathbf{X})
in
Definition

2.3
,
\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{S}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{X}
is an
(\epsilon,\delta)
-approximation of
\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}
.

Lemma

A.3
corresponds to the special case
\mathbf{C}=0
of the regularized formulation in
[
42
, Lemma 2.7]
. It establishes explicit conditions on the sample size
m
under which
\mathbf{S}\mathbf{X}
forms an
(\epsilon,\delta)
-subspace embedding of
\mathbf{X}
. This result serves as a technical foundation, ensuring that the sketch
\mathbf{S}\mathbf{X}
is well behaved throughout the subsequent analysis.

Appendix B
Proof of Theorem
4.2

We begin with the following result from
[
16
]
, which is used in the proof of
Theorem

4.2
.

Lemma B.1

(
[
16
, Lemma 36]
)
.

There is a universal constant
C>0
such that for any positive integer
b
, if
x\sim
Binomial
(b,0.5)
then,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\frac{1}{x+b/2}\Big]\geq\Big(1+\frac{1}{Cb}\Big)\cdot\frac{1}{b}.

For notational convenience, in this proof we will denote
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\mathrm{OLS}}
by
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}
. Consider a matrix
\mathbf{X}
whose first
2p
rows are given as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}_{[1:2p]}=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{1}{2}&0&0&\cdots&0\\
\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}&0&0&\cdots&0\\
0&\frac{1}{2}&0&\cdots&0\\
0&\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}&0&\cdots&0\\
0&0&\frac{1}{2}&\cdots&0\\
0&0&\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}&\cdots&0\\
0&0&0&\cdots&\frac{1}{2}\\
0&0&0&\cdots&\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\\
\end{bmatrix}.

Also, if
n>2p
, let rows subsequent to
(2p)^{th}
row be all zeros. In addition, consider the response vector
\mathbf{y}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
as follows:

\displaystyle[\mathbf{y}]_{i}=\begin{cases}-1,\ \ &\text{$i\leq p$ and $i$ is odd,}\\
1,\ \ &\text{$i\leq p$ and $i$ is even,}\\
1,\ \ &\text{$p<i\leq 2p$,}\\
0,\ \ &\text{$i>2p$}.\end{cases}

Note that
\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{I}_{p}
, the exact least squares estimator
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}
can be found as
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}=\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{y}
.
The entries of
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}
are given as

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}_{i}=\begin{cases}\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}-\frac{1}{2},\ \ \text{$i\leq\frac{p}{2}$},\\
\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}+\frac{1}{2},\ \ \text{$\frac{p}{2}<i\leq p$}.\end{cases}

Also, note that
\|\mathbf{y}\|^{2}=2p
,
\|\mathbf{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}-\mathbf{y}\|^{2}=\Theta(p)
,
\|\mathbf{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}\|^{2}=\Theta(p)
. The exact leverage scores of
\mathbf{X}
lie in the set
\{\frac{1}{4},\frac{3}{4},0\}
. Consider the probability distribution
\{\pi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
, where
\pi_{i}=\frac{1}{2p}
for
i\leq 2p
and
\pi_{i}=0
for
i>2p
. Note that
\{\pi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
, provides a
\frac{1}{2}
-approximation to exact leverage score sampling. Let
m\geq p
,
\mathbf{S}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m\times n}
be sampling matrix sampled from the distribution
\{\pi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
. Let
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=(\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{S}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{X})^{\dagger}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{S}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{y}
be the sketched least squares estimator and
\bar{\boldsymbol{\beta}}={\mathbb{E}}[\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}]
. We can specify the distribution of every entry in
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
. To see that, we start by noting that
\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{S}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{X}
is a diagonal matrix where

\displaystyle[\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{S}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{X}]_{ii}=\frac{3}{4}\cdot s_{2i}+\frac{1}{4}\cdot s_{2i-1},\ \ \text{for}\ \ 1\leq i\leq p.

where
s_{2i}
and
s_{2i-1}
denote the number of times
(2i)^{th}
and
(2i-1)^{th}
rows are selected while sampling
\mathbf{S}
from
\{\pi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle[(\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{S}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{X})^{\dagger}]_{ii}=\begin{cases}\frac{4}{3s_{2i}+s_{2i-1}},\ \ &\text{if $s_{2i}+s_{2i-1}>0$,}\\
0,\ \ &\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

The
i^{th}
entry of
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
is given as

\displaystyle[\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}]_{i}=\begin{cases}\frac{2(\sqrt{3}s_{2i}-s_{2i-1})}{3s_{2i}+s_{2i-1}},\ \ \ &\text{$i\leq\frac{p}{2}$ and $s_{2i}+s_{2i-1}>0$,}\\
0,\ \ \ &\text{$i\leq\frac{p}{2}$ and $s_{2i}+s_{2i-1}=0$,}\\
\frac{2(\sqrt{3}s_{2i}+s_{2i-1})}{3s_{2i}+s_{2i-1}},\ \ \ &\text{$\frac{p}{2}<i\leq p$ and $s_{2i}+s_{2i-1}>0$,}\\
0,\ \ \ &\text{$\frac{p}{2}<i\leq p$ and $s_{2i}+s_{2i-1}=0$}.\end{cases}

Note that, for
1\leq i\leq\frac{p}{2}
, the distribution of
[\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}]_{i}^{\prime}s
are identical, and for
i>\frac{p}{2}
, the distribution of
[\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}]_{i}^{\prime}s
are identical. Due to this observation,
{\mathbb{E}}[\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}]_{i}={\mathbb{E}}[\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}]_{1}
for all
i\leq\frac{p}{2}
and
{\mathbb{E}}[\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}]_{i}={\mathbb{E}}[\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}]_{p}
for all
i>\frac{p}{2}
. We first provide an upper bound on
{\mathbb{E}}[\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}]_{1}
. Let
\zeta
denote the event
\{s_{2i}+s_{2i-1}>0,\forall i\}
. We have,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}[\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}]_{1}={\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}\Big[\frac{2(\sqrt{3}s_{2}-s_{1})}{3s_{2}+s_{1}}\Big]={\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}\Big[\frac{2\big((1+\sqrt{3})s_{2}-(s_{1}+s_{2})\big)}{2s_{2}+(s_{1}+s_{2})}\Big].

Let
b:=s_{1}+s_{2}
and
x:=s_{2}
, we get

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}[\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}]_{1}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}\Big[\frac{2\big((1+\sqrt{3})x-b\big)}{2x+b}\Big]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}\Big[\frac{(1+\sqrt{3})(2x+b-b)}{2x+b}-\frac{2b}{2x+b}\Big]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}\Big[1+\sqrt{3}-\frac{(3+\sqrt{3})b}{2x+b}\Big]

\displaystyle=(1+\sqrt{3})\cdot{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}\Big[1-\frac{b\sqrt{3}}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{x+b/2}\Big].

Note that for any given fixed
b>0
,
q\sim\text{Binomial}(x,0.5)
. Using Lemma
B.1
, we get
{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\frac{1}{x+b/2}\Big]\geq\Big(1+\frac{1}{Cb}\Big)\cdot\frac{1}{b}
for some universal constant
C
. Therefore,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}[\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}]_{1}

\displaystyle\leq(1+\sqrt{3})\cdot{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}\Big[1-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\big(1+\frac{1}{Cb}\big)\Big]

\displaystyle=(1+\sqrt{3})\cdot\Big(1-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2C}\cdot{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}\Big[\frac{1}{b}\Big]\Big)

\displaystyle=(1+\sqrt{3})\cdot\Big(1-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2C}\cdot\sum_{t=1}^{m}{\frac{1}{t}\Pr(b=t)}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq(1+\sqrt{3})\cdot\Big(1-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2C}\cdot\sum_{t=1}^{2m/p}{\frac{1}{t}\Pr(b=t)}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq(1+\sqrt{3})\cdot\Big(1-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}-\frac{p\sqrt{3}}{4mC}\cdot\sum_{t=1}^{2m/p}{\Pr(b=t)}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq(1+\sqrt{3})\cdot\Big(1-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}-\frac{p\sqrt{3}}{8mC}\Big),

where the last inequality holds because
\sum_{t=1}^{2m/p}{\Pr(b=t)}\geq\frac{1}{2}
, as
b\sim\text{Binomial}(m,\frac{1}{p})
. Therefore, we get,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}[\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}]_{1}

\displaystyle\leq 1-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}+\sqrt{3}-\frac{3}{2}-\frac{3+\sqrt{3}}{8C}\cdot\frac{p}{m}=\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{3+\sqrt{3}}{8C}\cdot\frac{p}{m}

\displaystyle=[\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}]_{1}-\frac{3+\sqrt{3}}{8C}\cdot\frac{p}{m}.

Similarly, we can show that,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}[\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}]_{p}\geq\Big([\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}]_{p}+\frac{3-\sqrt{3}}{8C}\cdot\frac{p}{m}\Big).

Therefore, we get

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}[\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}]_{i}

\displaystyle=\Big([\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}]_{i}-c_{1}\Big)

for
i\leq\frac{p}{2}
,

\displaystyle=\Big([\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}]_{i}+c_{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\text{for $i>\frac{p}{2}$},

for some
c_{1},c_{2}=\Omega(\frac{p}{m})
. Let
\bar{\boldsymbol{\beta}}={\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}[\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}]
and
\gamma
be any scalar factor. We have,

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{X}\gamma\bar{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\mathbf{y}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\|\mathbf{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}-\mathbf{y}\|^{2}+\|\mathbf{X}(\gamma\bar{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\|\mathbf{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}-\mathbf{y}\|^{2}+\|\mathbf{X}_{[1:p,1:\frac{p}{2}]}(\gamma\bar{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{[1:\frac{p}{2}]}-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}_{[1:\frac{p}{2}]})\|^{2}

\displaystyle+\|\mathbf{X}_{[p+1:2p,\frac{p}{2}+1:p]}(\gamma\bar{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{[\frac{p}{2}+1:p]}-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}_{[\frac{p}{2}+1:p]})\|^{2},

(12)

where by
\mathbf{X}_{[p+1:2p,\frac{p}{2}+1:p]}
we mean the sub matrix of
\mathbf{X}
formed by
(p+1)^{th}
to
(2p)^{th}
rows and
(\frac{p}{2}+1)^{th}
to
p^{th}
columns. The other subscripts can be understood similarly. First, consider the term
\|\mathbf{X}_{[1:p,1:\frac{p}{2}]}(\gamma\bar{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{[1:\frac{p}{2}]}-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}_{[1:\frac{p}{2}]})\|^{2}
. We get,

\displaystyle\Big\|\mathbf{X}_{[1:p,1:\frac{p}{2}]}(\gamma\bar{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{[1:\frac{p}{2}]}-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}_{[1:\frac{p}{2}]})\Big\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\Big\|\mathbf{X}_{[1:p,1:\frac{p}{2}]}\big(\gamma\cdot(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}_{[1:\frac{p}{2}]}-c_{1}\mathbf{1})-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}_{[1:\frac{p}{2}]}\big)\Big\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\Big\|\mathbf{X}_{[1:p,1:\frac{p}{2}]}\big(\gamma\cdot c_{1}\mathbf{1}+(1-\gamma)\cdot\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}_{[1:\frac{p}{2}]}\big)\Big\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\Big(\gamma\cdot c_{1}+(1-\gamma)\cdot[\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}]_{1}\Big)^{2}\cdot\|\mathbf{X}_{[1:p,1:\frac{p}{2}]}\mathbf{1}\|^{2},

where
\mathbf{1}
denotes a vector of
1
’s. As
\|\mathbf{X}_{[1:p,1:\frac{p}{2}]}\mathbf{1}\|^{2}=\Theta(p)
, and also recall
\|\mathbf{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}-\mathbf{y}\|^{2}=\Theta(p)
, we get

\displaystyle\Big\|\mathbf{X}_{[1:p,1:\frac{p}{2}]}(\gamma\bar{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{[1:\frac{p}{2}]}-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}_{[1:\frac{p}{2}]})\Big\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\Big(\gamma\cdot c_{1}+(1-\gamma)\cdot[\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}]_{1}\Big)^{2}\cdot\Theta(\|\mathbf{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}-\mathbf{y}\|^{2}).

If
0<\gamma\leq 1
, then we get

\displaystyle\Big(\gamma\cdot c_{1}+(1-\gamma)\cdot[\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}]_{1}\Big)^{2}>\max\Big\{\gamma^{2}\cdot\Omega\big(\frac{p^{2}}{m^{2}}\big),(1-\gamma)^{2}\cdot[\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}]_{1}^{2}\Big\}.

At least one of
\gamma
or
1-\gamma
is at least
\frac{1}{2}
. In either case we get,

\displaystyle\Big(\gamma\cdot c_{1}+(1-\gamma)\cdot[\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}]_{1}\Big)^{2}=\Omega\big(\frac{p^{2}}{m^{2}}\big).

Therefore, the theorem holds for any
0<\gamma\leq 1
. In case
\gamma<0
or
\gamma\geq 1
, consider the last term in relation (
12
). We have,

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{X}_{[p+1:2p,\frac{p}{2}+1:p]}(\gamma\bar{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{[\frac{p}{2}+1:p]}-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}_{[\frac{p}{2}+1:p]})\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\|\mathbf{X}_{[p+1:2p,\frac{p}{2}+1:p]}(\gamma\cdot(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}+c_{2}\mathbf{1})_{[\frac{p}{2}+1:p]}-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}_{[\frac{p}{2}+1:p]})\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\|\mathbf{X}_{[p+1:2p,\frac{p}{2}+1:p]}(\gamma\cdot c_{2}\mathbf{1}-(1-\gamma)\cdot\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}_{[\frac{p}{2}+1:p]})\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\Big(\gamma\cdot c_{2}-(1-\gamma)\cdot[\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}]_{p}\Big)^{2}\cdot\Theta(\|\mathbf{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}-\mathbf{y}\|^{2})

\displaystyle=\Omega\big(\frac{p^{2}}{m^{2}}\big)\cdot\Theta(\|\mathbf{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}-\mathbf{y}\|^{2}).

The last inequality holds as either
\gamma<0
or
\gamma\geq 1
.
This completes the proof of Theorem
4.2

Appendix C
Proof of Theorem
4.3

Let
\mathbf{U}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}
has orthonormal columns spanning the column space of
\mathbf{X}
. Then,
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\mathrm{OLS}}=\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{y}
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=(\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{S}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{U})^{\dagger}\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{S}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{y}
. Furthermore,

\displaystyle L(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}})-L(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\mathrm{OLS}})

\displaystyle=\big\|\mathbf{U}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\mathrm{OLS}})\big\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\big\|\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\mathrm{OLS}}\big\|^{2}.

Let
\zeta
denote the event that

\displaystyle(1+\epsilon)^{-1}\cdot\mathbf{I}\preceq\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{S}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{U}\preceq(1+\epsilon)\cdot\mathbf{I},

for
\epsilon=\sqrt{\frac{3p\theta_{\max}(\mathbf{X})\log(2p/\delta)}{m}}
. First, note that for
m>12p\theta_{\max}\log(2p/\delta)
,
\Pr(\zeta)\geq 1-\delta
. This claim is essentially the subspace embedding guarantee provided by the approximate leverage sampling matrix
\mathbf{S}
(Lemma
A.3
). In what follows, we will upper bound
{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}[L(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}})-L(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\mathrm{OLS}})]
. We have,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}[L(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}})-L(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\mathrm{OLS}})]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}[\|\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\mathrm{OLS}}\|^{2}]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}\Big[\Big\|(\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{S}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{U})^{-1}\big(\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{S}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{y}-(\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{S}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{U})\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\mathrm{OLS}}\big)\Big\|^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}\Big[\Big\|(\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{S}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{U})^{-1}\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{S}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\big(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{U}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\mathrm{OLS}}\big)\Big\|^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}\Big[\big\|(\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{S}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{U})^{-1}\big\|^{2}\cdot\big\|\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{S}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{r}\big\|^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq(1+3\epsilon)^{2}\cdot{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}\Big[\|\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{S}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{r}\||^{2}\Big],

where
\mathbf{r}
denotes
\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{U}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\mathrm{OLS}}
. The remaining term to upper bound is
{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}\Big[\|\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{S}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{r}\|^{2}\Big]
. It is straightforward to show that
{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\|\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{S}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{r}\|^{2}]=\mathbf{r}^{\top}\diag\Big(\frac{\ell_{i}(\mathbf{X})}{m\pi_{i}}\Big)\mathbf{r}
. Note that,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\|\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{S}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{r}\|^{2}\Big]={\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}\Big[\|\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{S}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{r}\|^{2}\Big]\cdot\Pr(\zeta)+{\mathbb{E}}_{\neg\zeta}\Big[\|\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{S}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{r}\|^{2}\Big]\cdot\Pr(\neg\zeta).

Therefore,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}\Big[\|\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{S}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{r}\|^{2}\Big]\leq\frac{1}{\Pr(\zeta)}\cdot\mathbf{r}^{\top}\diag\Big(\frac{\ell_{i}(\mathbf{X})}{m\pi_{i}}\Big)\mathbf{r}.

Combining everything we get,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}[L(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}})-L(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\mathrm{OLS}})]

\displaystyle\leq(1+3\epsilon)^{2}\cdot\frac{1}{\Pr(\zeta)}\cdot\mathbf{r}^{\top}\diag\Big\{\frac{\ell_{i}(\mathbf{X})}{m\pi_{i}}\Big\}_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{r}

\displaystyle\leq(1+15\epsilon)\cdot\frac{1}{\Pr(\zeta)}\cdot\mathbf{r}^{\top}\diag\Big\{\frac{\ell_{i}(\mathbf{X})}{m\pi_{i}}\Big\}_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{r}

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\Delta+15\epsilon^{\prime}\|\mathbf{r}\|^{2}\Big)\cdot\frac{1}{\Pr(\zeta)},

where
\Delta=\mathbf{r}^{\top}\diag\Big\{\frac{\ell_{i}(\mathbf{X})}{m\pi_{i}}\Big\}_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{r}
and
\epsilon^{\prime}=\sqrt{\frac{3p^{3}\theta^{3}_{\max}(\mathbf{X})\log(2p/\delta)}{m^{3}}}
. This proves Theorem
4.3
.

Appendix D
Proof of Theorem
4.4

In this section, we first provide RMT intuition for the bias of the debiased estimator in
Section

D.1
.
The corresponding intuition for the variance follows analogously and is omitted for brevity.
We then present the detailed proof of
Theorem

4.4
in
Section

D.2
and
Section

D.3
.
We next provide the proofs of auxiliary results related to
Theorem

4.4
in
Section

D.4
and
Section

D.5
, with additional lemmas from
[
42
]
deferred to
Section

D.6
.

D.1
RMT intuition for Theorem
4.4

In this subsection, we provide a heuristic derivation of the bias of the debiased estimator in
Theorem

4.4
.
We begin by recalling some notations from
Theorem

4.4
.
Let
\mathbf{z}^{\top}_{s}=\mathbf{e}^{\top}_{i_{s}}/\sqrt{\pi_{i_{s}}}\mathbf{X}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
and
\mathbf{w}_{s}^{\top}=\mathbf{e}^{\top}_{i_{s}}/\sqrt{\pi_{i_{s}}}
so that
{\mathbb{E}}[\mathbf{z}_{s}{\mathbf{z}}^{\top}_{s}]=\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}
and
{\mathbb{E}}[\mathbf{z}_{s}\mathbf{w}_{s}^{\top}]=\mathbf{X}^{\top}
.
Denote

\mathbf{Q}=(\mathbf{X}^{\top}\check{\mathbf{S}}^{\top}\check{\mathbf{S}}\mathbf{X})^{-1}=\left(\sum^{m}_{s=1}\frac{1}{m}F_{i_{s},i_{s}}\mathbf{z}_{s}{\mathbf{z}}^{\top}_{s}\right)^{-1},~~\mathbf{Q}_{-s}=\left(\sum_{k\neq s}\frac{1}{m}F_{i_{s},i_{s}}\mathbf{z}_{k}{\mathbf{z}}^{\top}_{k}\right)^{-1},

where
\mathbf{Q}_{-s}
is independent of
\mathbf{z}_{s}
, and
F_{i_{s},i_{s}}
is a deterministic term whose explicit form will be specified later.
Recalling
\rm{rank}(\mathbf{X})=\mathit{p}
, we rewrite the estimator
(\check{\mathbf{S}}\mathbf{X})^{\dagger}\check{\mathbf{S}}=\mathbf{Q}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\check{\mathbf{S}}^{\top}\check{\mathbf{S}}
and the pseudoinverse
\mathbf{X}^{\dagger}=(\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{X}^{\top}
.
Here, our objective is to choose the diagonal entries
F_{ii}
such that
{\mathbb{E}}[\mathbf{Q}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\check{\mathbf{S}}^{\top}\check{\mathbf{S}}]\simeq\mathbf{X}^{\dagger}
.
Considering

\sum^{m}_{s=1}\frac{1}{m}{\mathbb{E}}[F_{i_{s}i_{s}}\mathbf{z}_{s}{\mathbf{z}}^{\top}_{s}]=\sum^{n}_{i=1}F_{ii}{\mathbf{x}}_{{i}}{\mathbf{x}}_{{i}}^{\top},

together with Sherman-Morrison formula in
Lemma

A.1
,
we first obtain

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathbf{Q}}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\check{\mathbf{S}}^{\top}\check{\mathbf{S}}]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left[\frac{{\mathbf{Q}}_{-s}F_{i_{s}i_{s}}\mathbf{z}_{s}\mathbf{w}_{s}^{\top}}{1+F_{i_{s}i_{s}}\mathbf{z}_{s}^{\top}{\mathbf{Q}}_{-s}\mathbf{z}_{s}/m}\right]=\sum^{n}_{i=1}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\frac{{\mathbf{Q}}_{-s}F_{ii}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{e}_{i}\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}}{1+F_{ii}\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{X}{\mathbf{Q}}_{-s}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{e}_{i}/m\pi_{i}}\right]

Let
\ell_{i}(\mathbf{X})=\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{X}(\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{e}_{i}
the
i^{\rm{th}}
leverage score of
\mathbf{X}
. Applying the rank-one perturbation formula for matrix inversion (see, e.g.,
[
48
, Lemma 2.6]
), we obtain

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathbf{Q}}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\check{\mathbf{S}}^{\top}\check{\mathbf{S}}]

\displaystyle\simeq\sum^{n}_{i=1}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\frac{{\mathbf{Q}}_{-s}F_{ii}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{e}_{i}\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}}{1+F_{ii}\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{X}(\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{e}_{i}/m\pi_{i}}\right]\simeq{\mathbb{E}}[\mathbf{Q}]\mathbf{X}^{\top}\sum^{n}_{i=1}\frac{F_{ii}\mathbf{e}_{i}\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}}{1+F_{ii}\ell_{i}(\mathbf{X})/m\pi_{i}}.

We now choose

\displaystyle F_{ii}=\frac{m\pi_{i}}{m\pi_{i}-\ell_{i}(\mathbf{X})}

(13)

so that

\displaystyle\sum^{n}_{i=1}\frac{F_{ii}\mathbf{e}_{i}\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}}{1+F_{ii}\ell_{i}(\mathbf{X})/m\pi_{i}}=\mathbf{I}_{n}.

Combined with Proposition 3.2 in
[
42
]
and the choice of

F_{ii}
in (
13
), which together imply that
{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathbf{Q}}]\simeq(\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X})^{-1}
,
we get

{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathbf{Q}}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\check{\mathbf{S}}^{\top}\check{\mathbf{S}}]\simeq(\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{X}^{\top}.

This choice leads to the following debiased sampling matrix

\displaystyle\check{\mathbf{S}}=\diag\left\{\sqrt{m\pi_{i_{s}}/(m\pi_{i_{s}}-\ell_{i_{s}}(\mathbf{X}))}\right\}^{m}_{s=1}\cdot\mathbf{S},\quad i_{s}\in\{1,\ldots,n\}.

D.2
Detailed proof of the bias of debiased estimator in Theorem
4.4

For ease of the subsequent analysis, we first rewrite the bias of the debiased estimator:

\displaystyle\textbf{Bias}(\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}})

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}[\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}}]^{\top}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}[\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}}]-2\mathbf{y}^{\top}\mathbf{X}{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}[\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}}]-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{OLS}^{\top}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{OLS}+2\mathbf{y}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{OLS}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}[\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}}]^{\top}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}[\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}}]-2\boldsymbol{\beta}_{OLS}^{\top}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}[\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}}]-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{OLS}^{\top}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{OLS}+2\boldsymbol{\beta}_{OLS}^{\top}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{OLS}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}[\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}}]^{\top}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}[\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}}]-2\boldsymbol{\beta}_{OLS}^{\top}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}[\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}}]+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{OLS}^{\top}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{OLS}

\displaystyle=({\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}[\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}}]-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{OLS})^{\top}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}({\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}[\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}}]-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{OLS})=({\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}[\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}}]-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{OLS})^{\top}\mathbf{H}({\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}[\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}}]-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{OLS})

\displaystyle=\Big\|\mathbf{H}^{\frac{1}{2}}({\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}[\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}}]-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{OLS})\Big\|^{2},

with
\mathbf{H}=\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}
. This reformulation shows that the proof of the bias bound for the debiased estimator proceeds in two main steps:

1.

construct a high probability event
\zeta
, based on subspace-embedding-type results in
Lemma

A.3
, under which the inverse
(\mathbf{X}^{\top}\check{\mathbf{S}}^{\top}\check{\mathbf{S}}\mathbf{X})^{-1}
is well conditioned; and

2.

conditioned on that event
\zeta
, bound the quantity
\|\mathbf{H}^{1/2}({\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}[\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}}]-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{OLS})\|^{2}
using a “leave-one-out” type analysis.

We begin by constructing a high-probability event
\zeta
.
Without loss of generality, assume that
t=m/4
is an integer. We partition the index set
\{1,\ldots,m\}
into four blocks of size
t
, and define the events:

\displaystyle\zeta_{j}:\sum^{tj}_{s=t(j-1)+1}\frac{1}{t}\mathbf{z}_{s}{\mathbf{z}}^{\top}_{s}\succeq\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X},~~~j=1,2,3,4,~~~\zeta=\bigcap^{4}_{j=1}\zeta_{j}.

(14)

By
F_{i_{s}i_{s}}\geq 1
, each event
\zeta_{j}
further implies

\displaystyle\sum^{tj}_{s=t(j-1)+1}\frac{1}{t}F_{i_{s}i_{s}}\mathbf{z}_{s}{\mathbf{z}}^{\top}_{s}\succeq\sum^{tj}_{s=t(j-1)+1}\frac{1}{t}\mathbf{z}_{s}{\mathbf{z}}^{\top}_{s}\succeq\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X},~~~j=1,2,3,4.

Intuitively, the event
\zeta_{j}
ensures that the (weighted) average of the rank-one matrices
\mathbf{z}_{s}\mathbf{z}_{s}^{\top}
over the
j
-th block forms a sketch of size
t
that provides a “lower”
1/2
-spectral-approximation of
\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}
, in the sense of
Definition

2.4
.

Under random sampling in
Definition

2.1
, the events
\zeta_{1}
,
\zeta_{2}
,
\zeta_{3}
,
\zeta_{4}
are mutually independent. Consequently, for any index
s\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
, there exists a block index
j=j(s)\in\{1,2,3,4\}
such that

1.

the event
\zeta_{j}
is independent of
\mathbf{z}_{s}
; and

2.

conditioning on
\zeta_{j}
, it follows that
\mathbf{Q}\preceq\mathbf{Q}_{-s}\preceq 8\mathbf{H}^{-1}
.

Denote
\check{\mathbf{z}}^{\top}_{s}=\mathbf{e}^{\top}_{i_{s}}/\sqrt{\pi_{i_{s}}}\mathbf{X}_{\mathbf{H}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
with
\mathbf{X}_{\mathbf{H}}=\mathbf{X}\mathbf{H}^{-1/2}
, let

\check{\mathbf{Q}}=\mathbf{H}^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathbf{Q}\mathbf{H}^{\frac{1}{2}}=(\mathbf{X}_{\mathbf{H}}^{\top}\check{\mathbf{S}}^{\top}\check{\mathbf{S}}\mathbf{X}_{\mathbf{H}})^{-1}=\left(\sum^{m}_{s=1}\frac{1}{m}F_{i_{s},i_{s}}\check{\mathbf{z}}_{s}\check{\mathbf{z}}^{\top}_{s}\right)^{-1},

and

\check{\mathbf{Q}}_{-s}=\mathbf{H}^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathbf{Q}_{-s}\mathbf{H}^{\frac{1}{2}}=\left(\sum_{k\neq s}\frac{1}{m}F_{i_{k},i_{k}}\check{\mathbf{z}}_{k}\check{\mathbf{z}}^{\top}_{k}\right)^{-1}.

Letting
\mathbf{r}=\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{OLS}
and
\check{a}_{s}=\mathbf{e}^{\top}_{i_{s}}/\sqrt{\pi_{i_{s}}}\mathbf{r}
, we now rewrite

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}[\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}}]-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{OLS}={\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}[(\mathbf{X}^{\top}\check{\mathbf{S}}^{\top}\check{\mathbf{S}}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\check{\mathbf{S}}^{\top}\check{\mathbf{S}}\mathbf{r}],

which gives

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}^{\frac{1}{2}}({\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}[\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}}]-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{OLS})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{H}^{\frac{1}{2}}{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}[(\mathbf{X}^{\top}\check{\mathbf{S}}^{\top}\check{\mathbf{S}}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\check{\mathbf{S}}^{\top}\check{\mathbf{S}}\mathbf{r}]={\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}[(\mathbf{X}_{\mathbf{H}}^{\top}\check{\mathbf{S}}^{\top}\check{\mathbf{S}}\mathbf{X}_{\mathbf{H}})^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{\mathbf{H}}^{\top}\check{\mathbf{S}}^{\top}\check{\mathbf{S}}\mathbf{r}]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}[\check{\mathbf{Q}}\mathbf{X}_{\mathbf{H}}^{\top}\check{\mathbf{S}}^{\top}\check{\mathbf{S}}\mathbf{r}]=\frac{1}{m}\sum^{m}_{s=1}{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}[\check{\mathbf{Q}}F_{i_{s}i_{s}}\check{\mathbf{z}}_{s}\check{a}_{s}]={\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}\left[\frac{\check{\mathbf{Q}}_{-s}F_{i_{s}i_{s}}\check{\mathbf{z}}_{s}\check{a}_{s}}{1+\frac{1}{m}F_{i_{s}i_{s}}\check{\mathbf{z}}_{s}^{\top}\check{\mathbf{Q}}_{-s}\check{\mathbf{z}}_{s}}\right]

\displaystyle=\underbrace{{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}\left[\check{\mathbf{Q}}_{-s}\check{\mathbf{z}}_{s}\check{a}_{s}\left(\frac{F_{i_{s}i_{s}}}{1+\frac{1}{m}F_{i_{s}i_{s}}\check{\mathbf{z}}_{s}^{\top}\check{\mathbf{Q}}_{-s}\check{\mathbf{z}}_{s}}-1\right)\right]}_{\mathbf{T}_{1}}+\underbrace{{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}[\check{\mathbf{Q}}_{-s}\check{\mathbf{z}}_{s}\check{a}_{s}]}_{\mathbf{T}_{2}}.

This decomposition yields

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{H}^{\frac{1}{2}}({\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta}[\check{\boldsymbol{\beta}}]-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{OLS})\|^{2}\leq\|\mathbf{T}_{1}\|^{2}+\|\mathbf{T}_{2}\|^{2}+2\|\mathbf{T}_{1}\|\|\mathbf{T}_{2}\|.

(15)

Consequently, to establish the bias bound for the debiased estimator in
Theorem

4.4
, it suffices to derive upper bounds on the norms
\|\mathbf{T}_{1}\|
and
\|\mathbf{T}_{2}\|
in (
15
).
We first consider to bound the term
\|\mathbf{T}_{1}\|
.
Without loss of generality, assume that the events
\zeta_{1}
,
\zeta_{2}
,
\zeta_{3}
are independent of
\mathbf{z}_{s}
. Define
