Appendix G
Additional numerical experiments and implementation details

In this section, we first provide implementation details for the numerical experiments on subsampled OLS and fast CUR decomposition in
Section

G.1
and
Section

G.2
. We then present additional numerical results for subsampled OLS in
Section

G.3
, followed by numerical results for fast CUR decomposition in
Section

G.4
.

G.1
Sketching matrices

Given a data matrix
\mathbf{X}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}
, leverage scores are estimated following the procedure described in
[
23
]
.
The LESS-uniform sketching matrix is generated according to the construction detailed in
[
15
, Section E.1]
. Implementation specifics, including those for the SRHT, follow
[
15
]
.

Experiments were conducted on a server with an AMD EPYC 7452 32-Core Processor, 256GB RAM, and NVIDIA GeForce RTX 3090 GPUs. Code is publicly available
at
https://github.com/chengmeiniu/debiased-oblique-projections.git
.

G.2
Datasets

The Flight Delay dataset used in
Section

G.3
is obtained from the U.S. Department of Transportation. The dataset contains 539747 U.S. domestic weekday flights in January 2025, with five recorded variables per flight: arrival delay (difference in minutes between scheduled and actual arrival time, and early arrivals show negative numbers), arrival taxi in time (in minutes), departure taxi out time (in minutes), departure delays (difference in minutes between scheduled and actual departure time, and early departures show negative numbers), and computer reservation system based elapsed time of the flight (in minutes; a measure for the distance of the flight). Following
[
37
]
, we take arrival delay as the response and the remaining four variables as predictors, and further augment the design with all quadratic terms and pairwise interactions, yielding 14 predictors in total.
All variables are standardized to zero mean and unit variance. In the experiments, we randomly select
n=2^{13}
samples from the full dataset to construct the regression design matrix.

The MSD (Million Song Year Prediction) dataset used in
Section

4.3
consists of 515,345 songs released between 1922 and 2011. Each song is represented by multiple audio segments, and each segment is described by 12 timbre features that capture perceptual properties such as brightness and spectral flatness.
Our goal is to predict the release year using the complete set of timbre-based descriptors. Following
[
37
]
, we model the logarithm of the release year as the response variable and use all derived timbre features as predictors. All predictors are standardized to zero mean and unit variance. In the experiments, we randomly sample
n=2^{14}
observations from the full dataset to form the regression design matrix.

The CIFAR-10 dataset used in
Section

G.4
contains 60,000 images of size
32\times 32\times 3
. Each image is flattened into a vector.
We construct the data matrix using
n=2^{13}
samples and
p=2^{11}
features.

The ImageNet dataset used in
Section

G.4
is based on ImageNet-64, a downsampled version of ImageNet. We use images from
Imagenet64_train_part1
, specifically
train_data_batch_1
. Each image has resolution
64\times 64\times 3
and is flattened into a vector representation.
We construct the data matrix using
n=2^{14}
samples and
p=2^{11}
features.

G.3
Additional numerical experiments for subsampled OLS

In this section, we use the Flight Delay (Airline) dataset, obtained from the website of the U.S. Department of Transportation
3
3

3

Airline On-Time Performance Data,
https://www.transtats.bts.gov/DL_SelectFields.asp?Table_ID=236
.
, to provide additional numerical evidence supporting our theoretical findings on classical and debiased subsampled OLS. The data matrix
\mathbf{X}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}
is constructed from the Airline dataset, and
\mathbf{y}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
denotes the corresponding response vector.

Following
Section

4.3
,
Figure

2
examines how the sketch size
m
affects the bias and variance of subsampled OLS on the Airline dataset. We report the bias by
(L(\hat{\mathbb{E}}[\boldsymbol{\beta}])-L(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\mathrm{OLS}}))/L(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\mathrm{OLS}})
and the variance by
(\hat{\mathbb{E}}[L(\boldsymbol{\beta}])]-L(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\mathrm{OLS}}))/L(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\mathrm{OLS}})
, where
L(\boldsymbol{\beta})=\|\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}\|^{2}
is as in
Equation

4
. We compare uniform sampling (
UNI
), approximate leverage score sampling (
Lev
),
SRHT
(see
Definition

4.6
), and LESS
[
15
,
28
]
, together with their debiased variants
DUNI
,
DLev
, and
DSRHT
, using exact leverage scores.

The results on the Airline dataset in
Figure

2
closely match those on the MSD dataset in
Figure

1
. UNI is the most biased, while DUNI substantially reduces its bias and, approaches the comparable bias levels of Lev, DLev, SRHT, DSRHT, and LESS.
Debiasing has almost no visible effect on approximate leverage score sampling and SRHT, consistent with
Corollary

4.5
and
Corollary

4.7
. For variance, approximate leverage sampling, SRHT, their debiased variants, and LESS are comparable and uniformly outperform UNI and DUNI. Moreover, the debiased sampling schemes introduce essentially no variance increase relative to their corresponding standard counterparts. These results further support that debiasing improves accuracy without sacrificing variance. As expected, the standard non-debiased methods are also computationally cheaper; accordingly,
Table

3
reports timing results only for the standard schemes on the Airline and MSD datasets, where UNI, Lev, and SRHT are more efficient than LESS.

G.4
Numerical results for fast CUR decomposition

In this section, we present numerical experiments for fast CUR decomposition to support our theoretical findings on both classical and debiased CUR estimators. We evaluate CUR approximations on matrices
\mathbf{X}
constructed from the CIFAR-10 dataset
[
35
]
and the ImageNet dataset
[
12
]
.

In
Figure

3
and
Figure

4
, we study the effect of sketch size
m
on the bias and variance of fast CUR decomposition on CIFAR-10 and ImageNet datasets. We measure the bias by

\|\hat{\mathbb{E}}[\mathbf{X}-\mathbf{C}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{R}]\|_{F}^{2}/\|\mathbf{X}\|_{F}^{2}
and the variance by
\hat{\mathbb{E}}[\|\mathbf{X}-\mathbf{C}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{R}\|_{F}^{2}]/\|\mathbf{X}\|_{F}^{2}
.
We compare uniform sampling (
UNI
), approximate leverage score sampling (
Lev
), and
SRHT
(see
Definition

4.6
), together with their debiased counterparts
DUNI
,
DLev
, and
DSRHT
, using exact leverage scores.
For each method, we first sample columns and rows of
\mathbf{X}
to form
\mathbf{C}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times c}
and
\mathbf{R}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{r\times p}
, using column-norm and row-norm sampling probabilities, respectively. We fix
c=30
and
r=60
for CIFAR-10, and
c=40
and
r=100
for ImageNet. We then compute the core matrix
\mathbf{U}
in (
10
) and (
11
), with the standard sampling matrices

\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m_{c}\times n}
and
\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{R}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m_{r}\times p}
, and the debiased sampling matrices

\check{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m_{c}\times n}
and
\check{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathbf{R}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m_{r}\times p}
.

Table

4
uses the same choices of
c
and
r
as in
Figure

3
and
Figure

4
.

As shown in
Figure

3
and
Figure

4
, Lev and SRHT, together with their debiased variants, consistently outperform UNI and DUNI in the bias–variance performance, with SRHT achieving the best overall variance both with and without debiasing. Approximate leverage score sampling exhibits nearly identical bias and variance with and without debiasing; the same behavior is observed for SRHT. This is consistent with
Corollary

F.2
and
Corollary

F.3
.
In contrast, DUNI consistently reduces the bias of UNI, moving its performance closer to that of Lev, DLev, SRHT, and DSRHT, while maintaining nearly the same variance. Overall, debiasing improves accuracy without degrading variance.

In
Table

4
, we compare the accuracy–efficiency trade-off of our method with the OSP methods of
[
43
, Algorithm 2]
on CIFAR-10 and ImageNet datasets, where accuracy is measured by
\hat{\mathbb{E}}[\|\mathbf{X}-\mathbf{C}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{R}\|_{F}^{2}/\|\mathbf{X}\|_{F}^{2}]
, denoted as variance in
Figure

3
and
Figure

4
. The reported wall-clock time includes the computation of

\mathbf{C}
,
\mathbf{R}
,
\tilde{\mathbf{U}}
in (
28
) and
\mathbf{C}\tilde{\mathbf{U}}\mathbf{R}
for Lev and SRHT, and the computation of
\mathbf{C}
,
\mathbf{R}
,
\mathbf{U}^{*}=\mathbf{X}_{R,C}^{\dagger}
and
\mathbf{C}\mathbf{U}^{*}\mathbf{R}
for OSP-SRHT and OSP-SS. These OSP methods use randomized column-pivoted QR with oversampling to improve the stability and accuracy of the core matrix estimator
\mathbf{U}^{\star}=\mathbf{X}_{R,C}^{\dagger}
. Specifically, for OSP, we implement randomized column-pivoted QR using SRHT and sparse sign sketches, denoted by
OSP-SRHT
and
OSP-SS
, respectively. Since leverage-based methods and SRHT exhibit essentially identical performance with and without debiasing, while the standard versions are computationally cheaper,
Table

4
reports only the standard methods (Lev and SRHT) alongside OSP-SRHT and OSP-SS.
The results show that Lev achieves a better accuracy–efficiency trade-off than both OSP baselines, indicating that our CUR construction effectively balances computational cost and numerical stability. Although SRHT is slower than OSP-SS because SRHT-based sketching is more expensive than sparse sign sketching, it is still more accurate. Moreover, SRHT consistently outperforms OSP-SRHT in terms of the overall accuracy–efficiency trade-off, further demonstrating the advantage of our approach.
